I showed up late and honestly not as prepared as I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. I kept thinking afterward, you know, these kids showed up expecting something from me, and I couldn’t quite… deliver the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have these repetitive thoughts or checking behaviors caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with people, I guess it’s less like big arguments and more that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nWork has slowed down a lot because of all the rechecking, and I’ve missed design milestones. That spills over, because I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I know that’s not exactly “family or friends,” but some of those people depend on me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting them down.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop that mattered to me—I was late and not as prepared as I should’ve been. I kept thinking afterward, “They came expecting me to be on top of it,” and I just felt embarrassed. So… yes, it affects relationships in that sense, maybe more through disappointing people than through conflict.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty upsetting, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it happens I can lose whole chunks of time to it.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I’ll be re-reading the same email or text again and again, or reopening a folder on my computer even though I just looked at it. And then sometimes before I leave the house, or before I start work, I’m just standing there replaying a conversation in my head, trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, I know it probably doesn’t make sense from the outside. I work with computers, so you’d think I’d be good at trusting what’s on the screen, but instead I get stuck checking it. So the distress is partly the worry, but also the time I lose and the feeling that I can’t just move on like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or compulsions that are unrelated to your work or daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only work stuff, although it kind of sneaks in while I’m working.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to think through a system design, or getting ready for a lesson, and suddenly I’m not thinking about that anymore—I’m thinking, did I send the wrong message to my ex-wife? Or did I do something careless with old family photos? And then I’m off in my head going back over it.\n\nAnd it can be community things too. I’ve always tried to be dependable—there was this neighborhood tech thing years ago where I helped people set up email, and anyway… I’ll get this thought that I let someone down somehow. Even if I’ve already checked or thought it through, it just keeps barging in. I know I’ve been over it already, but I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events because of these intrusive thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding social gatherings exactly… it’s more that I’ve had to pull back from things I used to be able to do.\n\nLike at work, everything’s slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and then I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions—ones I used to handle pretty reliably. And that makes me feel bad, because I don’t like being the person who cancels or shows up half-ready.\n\nThere was also this youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I was late and not really prepared the way I should’ve been. I kept thinking about it afterward, like… they were counting on me. So I guess it affects events, yes, but more through being delayed or not ready than me saying, “I’m not going.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I’d say it has. I mean, I still get through the day, I’m not completely falling apart, but it’s made things feel smaller, if that makes sense.\n\nWork is the clearest place. I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s not really like me. I used to be pretty dependable with mentoring sessions too, and lately I’ve had to postpone them because I’m just not ready or I’ve lost time going back over things.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop… I keep thinking about that one. I showed up late and felt unprepared, and I was embarrassed. It mattered to me, those kinds of things matter to me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting people down. So yes, it’s definitely affected my quality of life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like these thoughts and checking behaviors are interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does interfere, maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI mean, with relationships, it’s not like I’ve cut people off exactly, but I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably, and those were… I don’t know, they mattered to me. I liked being useful in that way. And when I’m slowed down from going back over things, I’m just not as present.\n\nWork has definitely suffered too. I’ve missed design milestones, which is embarrassing because I’ve always kind of prided myself on being steady. It reminds me of this youth coding workshop I helped with—I was late and not really prepared, and I felt awful about that. Those kids were there to learn, and I kept thinking afterward, “Why couldn’t I just get myself together?”\n\nSo yes, it’s gotten in the way of enjoying things. Even things I care about end up feeling stressful or spoiled.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that these thoughts and behaviors might be significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have considered that. I mean, I don’t always want to call it “significant,” because that makes it sound like I’ve lost control of everything, and I’m still functioning. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s… not how I usually operate. I’ve always been the person who could keep things moving. And mentoring, too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I hate that, because those weren’t just meetings on a calendar to me.\n\nAnd then there was this youth coding workshop—I know I’ve mentioned it, but it sticks with me. I was late, not really prepared, and I felt embarrassed. It mattered to me, helping with that. So yes, I guess when it starts affecting work and people who are counting on me, that’s daily life and relationships, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are making it hard for you to live the life you want to live?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “the life I want” sounds a little grand, but yes, it’s making things smaller than I want them to be.\n\nLike at work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that really bothers me. I used to be the person who stayed on top of those things. And mentoring too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I always liked that part, helping people come along. My first manager used to say you learn a topic twice when you teach it, which is true, actually, especially with coding—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nBut yes, it’s interfering. That youth coding workshop still sticks in my mind. I was late, I wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt embarrassed because it mattered to me. So… yes, it is making it harder to live the way I want to live.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical actions that you feel compelled to repeat, such as washing your hands or locking doors?\n\nPatient: Not so much washing my hands or locks, no… at least that’s not the main thing.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get stuck re-reading emails or texts, reopening the same folders, that kind of thing. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s strange, because with computers you learn to double-check things—version control, file paths, all of that—and that’s useful, but this feels different. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. Re-reading an email or a text, then reopening the folder again, then going back to the message… and before I leave the house or start work, I’ll replay a conversation in my head. Like I’m trying to make absolutely sure I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because in my line of work checking things is normal—you know, files, versions, whether something saved correctly—but this isn’t efficient checking. It’s more like I can’t move on until I feel certain, and that certainty doesn’t really come.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I mean, “the life I want” sounds a little grand, but yes, it’s making things smaller than I want them to be.\n\nLike at work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that really bothers me. I used to be the person who stayed on top of those things. And mentoring too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I always liked that part, helping people come along. My first manager used to say you learn a topic twice when you teach it, which is true, actually, especially with coding—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nBut yes, it’s interfering. That youth coding workshop still sticks in my mind. I was late, I wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt embarrassed because it mattered to me. So… yes, it is making it harder to live the way I want to live.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical actions that you feel compelled to repeat, such as washing your hands or locking doors?\n\nPatient: Not so much washing my hands or locks, no… at least that’s not the main thing.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get stuck re-reading emails or texts, reopening the same folders, that kind of thing. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s strange, because with computers you learn to double-check things—version control, file paths, all of that—and that’s useful, but this feels different. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. Re-reading an email or a text, then reopening the folder again, then going back to the message… and before I leave the house or start work, I’ll replay a conversation in my head. Like I’m trying to make absolutely sure I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because in my line of work checking things is normal—you know, files, versions, whether something saved correctly—but this isn’t efficient checking. It’s more like I can’t move on until I feel certain, and that certainty doesn’t really come.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think these thoughts and checking behaviors are causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones and had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle reliably. I also felt embarrassed when I was late and unprepared for a youth coding workshop that mattered to me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It’s not just annoying, it’s causing real problems.

My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, which is… embarrassing, honestly. I used to be pretty dependable with that sort of thing. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally handled reliably. I always liked mentoring, too—there’s something satisfying about helping someone understand a tricky bit of code, like when you finally see the logic click for them—but lately I haven’t been able to keep up with it the same way.

And outside work, that youth coding workshop still bothers me. I was late and not prepared the way I should’ve been, and it mattered to me, so I felt really embarrassed. So yes, it’s distressing, and it’s affecting my daily life.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main reason is I keep losing these big chunks of time doing things over and over in my head. Like I’ll re-read an email or a text several times, or reopen folders on my computer to check something I already checked. Before I leave the house or start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, trying to make sure I didn’t say something wrong or miss something important.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. And then suddenly half an hour is gone. I work with computers, so there are always files and messages, and it just sort of… spirals. I know that’s probably why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing these repetitive checks or replays?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it’s also more like this pressure that I can’t move on until I’ve checked. Like with an email, I’ll read it, then read it again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I miss something?” and suddenly I’m opening the same folder again or replaying a conversation in my head before I leave the house.\n\nIt’s not that I want to spend time doing it. I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. Then I look up and I’ve lost half an hour.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that keep coming back to you, aside from worrying about making mistakes in your work or daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only work mistakes exactly. It can be when I’m trying to focus on something completely different, like working through a system design, or even preparing a lesson—I used to be pretty good at getting into that kind of focused headspace.\n\nBut then this thought barges in, like, “What if I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife?” Or I’ll start wondering if I mishandled some family photos somehow, or whether I let someone in the community down. And I know I’ve already gone over it, sometimes more than once, but it doesn’t just pass. It kind of hooks me.\n\nSorry, that’s probably more detail than you asked for. It’s just… it pops up even when I’m trying to do something totally unrelated.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform specific physical actions, like tapping or checking things in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Not really tapping, no… not like a ritual with numbers or anything, at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more checking-type things. I’ll re-read an email or a text, then reopen the same folder on my computer to make sure I saw what I thought I saw. And before I leave the house or start work, I’ll get stuck replaying conversations in my head, like I’m trying to prove I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nI know you asked about physical actions—I guess the folder-checking is physical in a way, clicking back into it—but it doesn’t feel like a set order so much as I can’t move on until I’ve checked enough. Then I realize I’ve lost a big chunk of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these repetitive thoughts or checks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding.” It’s more like I put things off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking or second-guessing.\n\nAt work, that’s been a real problem. I’m in design work, and things that used to move along at a normal pace have slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions too, which bothers me because I used to be pretty reliable with that.\n\nThere was also this youth coding workshop—I cared about that one. I showed up late and honestly not as prepared as I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. I kept thinking afterward, you know, these kids showed up expecting something from me, and I couldn’t quite… deliver the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have these repetitive thoughts or checking behaviors caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with people, I guess it’s less like big arguments and more that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nWork has slowed down a lot because of all the rechecking, and I’ve missed design milestones. That spills over, because I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I know that’s not exactly “family or friends,” but some of those people depend on me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting them down.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop that mattered to me—I was late and not as prepared as I should’ve been. I kept thinking afterward, “They came expecting me to be on top of it,” and I just felt embarrassed. So… yes, it affects relationships in that sense, maybe more through disappointing people than through conflict.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty upsetting, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it happens I can lose whole chunks of time to it.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I’ll be re-reading the same email or text again and again, or reopening a folder on my computer even though I just looked at it. And then sometimes before I leave the house, or before I start work, I’m just standing there replaying a conversation in my head, trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, I know it probably doesn’t make sense from the outside. I work with computers, so you’d think I’d be good at trusting what’s on the screen, but instead I get stuck checking it. So the distress is partly the worry, but also the time I lose and the feeling that I can’t just move on like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or compulsions that are unrelated to your work or daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only work stuff, although it kind of sneaks in while I’m working.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to think through a system design, or getting ready for a lesson, and suddenly I’m not thinking about that anymore—I’m thinking, did I send the wrong message to my ex-wife? Or did I do something careless with old family photos? And then I’m off in my head going back over it.\n\nAnd it can be community things too. I’ve always tried to be dependable—there was this neighborhood tech thing years ago where I helped people set up email, and anyway… I’ll get this thought that I let someone down somehow. Even if I’ve already checked or thought it through, it just keeps barging in. I know I’ve been over it already, but I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events because of these intrusive thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding social gatherings exactly… it’s more that I’ve had to pull back from things I used to be able to do.\n\nLike at work, everything’s slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and then I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions—ones I used to handle pretty reliably. And that makes me feel bad, because I don’t like being the person who cancels or shows up half-ready.\n\nThere was also this youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I was late and not really prepared the way I should’ve been. I kept thinking about it afterward, like… they were counting on me. So I guess it affects events, yes, but more through being delayed or not ready than me saying, “I’m not going.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I’d say it has. I mean, I still get through the day, I’m not completely falling apart, but it’s made things feel smaller, if that makes sense.\n\nWork is the clearest place. I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s not really like me. I used to be pretty dependable with mentoring sessions too, and lately I’ve had to postpone them because I’m just not ready or I’ve lost time going back over things.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop… I keep thinking about that one. I showed up late and felt unprepared, and I was embarrassed. It mattered to me, those kinds of things matter to me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting people down. So yes, it’s definitely affected my quality of life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like these thoughts and checking behaviors are interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does interfere, maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI mean, with relationships, it’s not like I’ve cut people off exactly, but I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably, and those were… I don’t know, they mattered to me. I liked being useful in that way. And when I’m slowed down from going back over things, I’m just not as present.\n\nWork has definitely suffered too. I’ve missed design milestones, which is embarrassing because I’ve always kind of prided myself on being steady. It reminds me of this youth coding workshop I helped with—I was late and not really prepared, and I felt awful about that. Those kids were there to learn, and I kept thinking afterward, “Why couldn’t I just get myself together?”\n\nSo yes, it’s gotten in the way of enjoying things. Even things I care about end up feeling stressful or spoiled.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that these thoughts and behaviors might be significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have considered that. I mean, I don’t always want to call it “significant,” because that makes it sound like I’ve lost control of everything, and I’m still functioning. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s… not how I usually operate. I’ve always been the person who could keep things moving. And mentoring, too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I hate that, because those weren’t just meetings on a calendar to me.\n\nAnd then there was this youth coding workshop—I know I’ve mentioned it, but it sticks with me. I was late, not really prepared, and I felt embarrassed. It mattered to me, helping with that. So yes, I guess when it starts affecting work and people who are counting on me, that’s daily life and relationships, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are making it hard for you to live the life you want to live?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “the life I want” sounds a little grand, but yes, it’s making things smaller than I want them to be.\n\nLike at work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that really bothers me. I used to be the person who stayed on top of those things. And mentoring too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I always liked that part, helping people come along. My first manager used to say you learn a topic twice when you teach it, which is true, actually, especially with coding—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nBut yes, it’s interfering. That youth coding workshop still sticks in my mind. I was late, I wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt embarrassed because it mattered to me. So… yes, it is making it harder to live the way I want to live.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical actions that you feel compelled to repeat, such as washing your hands or locking doors?\n\nPatient: Not so much washing my hands or locks, no… at least that’s not the main thing.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get stuck re-reading emails or texts, reopening the same folders, that kind of thing. Those kids were there to learn, and I kept thinking afterward, “Why couldn’t I just get myself together?”\n\nSo yes, it’s gotten in the way of enjoying things. Even things I care about end up feeling stressful or spoiled.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that these thoughts and behaviors might be significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have considered that. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I am. It’s mostly professional, but it bleeds into personal responsibilities too.

At work, I’m just slower than I used to be. I’ve missed design milestones because I get stuck checking and rechecking instead of moving forward. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally would’ve handled reliably. That bothers me, because I’ve always liked being the person people could count on for that.

And then there was this youth coding workshop—I know that’s not exactly work, but it mattered to me. I showed up late and wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. It reminded me of when I first started programming and how much it meant when someone actually showed up prepared to teach. So… yes, I’m not meeting expectations the way I used to.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main reason is I keep losing these big chunks of time doing things over and over in my head. Like I’ll re-read an email or a text several times, or reopen folders on my computer to check something I already checked. Before I leave the house or start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, trying to make sure I didn’t say something wrong or miss something important.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. And then suddenly half an hour is gone. I work with computers, so there are always files and messages, and it just sort of… spirals. I know that’s probably why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing these repetitive checks or replays?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it’s also more like this pressure that I can’t move on until I’ve checked. Like with an email, I’ll read it, then read it again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I miss something?” and suddenly I’m opening the same folder again or replaying a conversation in my head before I leave the house.\n\nIt’s not that I want to spend time doing it. I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t make some serious mistake. Then I look up and I’ve lost half an hour.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that keep coming back to you, aside from worrying about making mistakes in your work or daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only work mistakes exactly. It can be when I’m trying to focus on something completely different, like working through a system design, or even preparing a lesson—I used to be pretty good at getting into that kind of focused headspace.\n\nBut then this thought barges in, like, “What if I sent the wrong message to my ex-wife?” Or I’ll start wondering if I mishandled some family photos somehow, or whether I let someone in the community down. And I know I’ve already gone over it, sometimes more than once, but it doesn’t just pass. It kind of hooks me.\n\nSorry, that’s probably more detail than you asked for. It’s just… it pops up even when I’m trying to do something totally unrelated.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform specific physical actions, like tapping or checking things in a particular order?\n\nPatient: Not really tapping, no… not like a ritual with numbers or anything, at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more checking-type things. I’ll re-read an email or a text, then reopen the same folder on my computer to make sure I saw what I thought I saw. And before I leave the house or start work, I’ll get stuck replaying conversations in my head, like I’m trying to prove I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nI know you asked about physical actions—I guess the folder-checking is physical in a way, clicking back into it—but it doesn’t feel like a set order so much as I can’t move on until I’ve checked enough. Then I realize I’ve lost a big chunk of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these repetitive thoughts or checks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding.” It’s more like I put things off because I know I’m going to get stuck checking or second-guessing.\n\nAt work, that’s been a real problem. I’m in design work, and things that used to move along at a normal pace have slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions too, which bothers me because I used to be pretty reliable with that.\n\nThere was also this youth coding workshop—I cared about that one. I showed up late and honestly not as prepared as I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. I kept thinking afterward, you know, these kids showed up expecting something from me, and I couldn’t quite… deliver the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have these repetitive thoughts or checking behaviors caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with people, I guess it’s less like big arguments and more that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nWork has slowed down a lot because of all the rechecking, and I’ve missed design milestones. That spills over, because I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I know that’s not exactly “family or friends,” but some of those people depend on me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting them down.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop that mattered to me—I was late and not as prepared as I should’ve been. I kept thinking afterward, “They came expecting me to be on top of it,” and I just felt embarrassed. So… yes, it affects relationships in that sense, maybe more through disappointing people than through conflict.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty upsetting, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it happens I can lose whole chunks of time to it.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I’ll be re-reading the same email or text again and again, or reopening a folder on my computer even though I just looked at it. And then sometimes before I leave the house, or before I start work, I’m just standing there replaying a conversation in my head, trying to convince myself I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, I know it probably doesn’t make sense from the outside. I work with computers, so you’d think I’d be good at trusting what’s on the screen, but instead I get stuck checking it. So the distress is partly the worry, but also the time I lose and the feeling that I can’t just move on like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or compulsions that are unrelated to your work or daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not only work stuff, although it kind of sneaks in while I’m working.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to think through a system design, or getting ready for a lesson, and suddenly I’m not thinking about that anymore—I’m thinking, did I send the wrong message to my ex-wife? Or did I do something careless with old family photos? And then I’m off in my head going back over it.\n\nAnd it can be community things too. I’ve always tried to be dependable—there was this neighborhood tech thing years ago where I helped people set up email, and anyway… I’ll get this thought that I let someone down somehow. Even if I’ve already checked or thought it through, it just keeps barging in. I know I’ve been over it already, but I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social gatherings or events because of these intrusive thoughts or checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding social gatherings exactly… it’s more that I’ve had to pull back from things I used to be able to do.\n\nLike at work, everything’s slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and then I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions—ones I used to handle pretty reliably. And that makes me feel bad, because I don’t like being the person who cancels or shows up half-ready.\n\nThere was also this youth coding workshop that mattered to me, and I was late and not really prepared the way I should’ve been. I kept thinking about it afterward, like… they were counting on me. So I guess it affects events, yes, but more through being delayed or not ready than me saying, “I’m not going.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I’d say it has. I mean, I still get through the day, I’m not completely falling apart, but it’s made things feel smaller, if that makes sense.\n\nWork is the clearest place. I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s not really like me. I used to be pretty dependable with mentoring sessions too, and lately I’ve had to postpone them because I’m just not ready or I’ve lost time going back over things.\n\nAnd there was this youth coding workshop… I keep thinking about that one. I showed up late and felt unprepared, and I was embarrassed. It mattered to me, those kinds of things matter to me, and I hate feeling like I’m letting people down. So yes, it’s definitely affected my quality of life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like these thoughts and checking behaviors are interfering with your ability to enjoy activities or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does interfere, maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI mean, with relationships, it’s not like I’ve cut people off exactly, but I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably, and those were… I don’t know, they mattered to me. I liked being useful in that way. And when I’m slowed down from going back over things, I’m just not as present.\n\nWork has definitely suffered too. I’ve missed design milestones, which is embarrassing because I’ve always kind of prided myself on being steady. It reminds me of this youth coding workshop I helped with—I was late and not really prepared, and I felt awful about that. Those kids were there to learn, and I kept thinking afterward, “Why couldn’t I just get myself together?”\n\nSo yes, it’s gotten in the way of enjoying things. Even things I care about end up feeling stressful or spoiled.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that these thoughts and behaviors might be significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have considered that. I mean, I don’t always want to call it “significant,” because that makes it sound like I’ve lost control of everything, and I’m still functioning. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that’s… not how I usually operate. I’ve always been the person who could keep things moving. And mentoring, too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I hate that, because those weren’t just meetings on a calendar to me.\n\nAnd then there was this youth coding workshop—I know I’ve mentioned it, but it sticks with me. I was late, not really prepared, and I felt embarrassed. It mattered to me, helping with that. So yes, I guess when it starts affecting work and people who are counting on me, that’s daily life and relationships, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are making it hard for you to live the life you want to live?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “the life I want” sounds a little grand, but yes, it’s making things smaller than I want them to be.\n\nLike at work, I’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and that really bothers me. I used to be the person who stayed on top of those things. And mentoring too—I’ve had to postpone sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. I always liked that part, helping people come along. My first manager used to say you learn a topic twice when you teach it, which is true, actually, especially with coding—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nBut yes, it’s interfering. That youth coding workshop still sticks in my mind. I was late, I wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt embarrassed because it mattered to me. So… yes, it is making it harder to live the way I want to live.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical actions that you feel compelled to repeat, such as washing your hands or locking doors?\n\nPatient: Not so much washing my hands or locks, no… at least that’s not the main thing.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get stuck re-reading emails or texts, reopening the same folders, that kind of thing. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s strange, because with computers you learn to double-check things—version control, file paths, all of that—and that’s useful, but this feels different. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. Re-reading an email or a text, then reopening the folder again, then going back to the message… and before I leave the house or start work, I’ll replay a conversation in my head. Like I’m trying to make absolutely sure I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because in my line of work checking things is normal—you know, files, versions, whether something saved correctly—but this isn’t efficient checking. It’s more like I can’t move on until I feel certain, and that certainty doesn’t really come.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking behaviors are causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just annoying, it’s causing real problems.\n\nMy work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, which is… embarrassing, honestly. I used to be pretty dependable with that sort of thing. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally handled reliably. I always liked mentoring, too—there’s something satisfying about helping someone understand a tricky bit of code, like when you finally see the logic click for them—but lately I haven’t been able to keep up with it the same way.\n\nAnd outside work, that youth coding workshop still bothers me. I was late and not prepared the way I should’ve been, and it mattered to me, so I felt really embarrassed. So yes, it’s distressing, and it’s affecting my daily life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts and checking behaviors are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially at work. I mean, that’s where it shows up the most clearly.\n\nI’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, which is hard to admit because I’ve always sort of prided myself on being reliable with that. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle without much trouble. I like those sessions, actually—helping someone get unstuck in code, it reminds me of when I was first learning and someone took the time with me—but lately I just haven’t been able to keep up the same way.\n\nRelationships… I don’t know if I’d say it’s destroyed anything, but it has affected how I show up for people. Like that youth coding workshop—I was late and unprepared, and that really mattered to me. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to fulfill responsibilities or meet expectations in your personal or professional life due to these thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. It’s mostly professional, but it bleeds into personal responsibilities too.\n\nAt work, I’m just slower than I used to be. I’ve missed design milestones because I get stuck checking and rechecking instead of moving forward. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally would’ve handled reliably. That bothers me, because I’ve always liked being the person people could count on for that.\n\nAnd then there was this youth coding workshop—I know that’s not exactly work, but it mattered to me. I showed up late and wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. It reminded me of when I first started programming and how much it meant when someone actually showed up prepared to teach. So… yes, I’m not meeting expectations the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So… yes, it is making it harder to live the way I want to live.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical actions that you feel compelled to repeat, such as washing your hands or locking doors?\n\nPatient: Not so much washing my hands or locks, no… at least that’s not the main thing.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get stuck re-reading emails or texts, reopening the same folders, that kind of thing. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s strange, because with computers you learn to double-check things—version control, file paths, all of that—and that’s useful, but this feels different. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. Re-reading an email or a text, then reopening the folder again, then going back to the message… and before I leave the house or start work, I’ll replay a conversation in my head. I always liked mentoring, too—there’s something satisfying about helping someone understand a tricky bit of code, like when you finally see the logic click for them—but lately I haven’t been able to keep up with it the same way.\n\nAnd outside work, that youth coding workshop still bothers me. I was late and not prepared the way I should’ve been, and it mattered to me, so I felt really embarrassed. So yes, it’s distressing, and it’s affecting my daily life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts and checking behaviors are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially at work. I like those sessions, actually—helping someone get unstuck in code, it reminds me of when I was first learning and someone took the time with me—but lately I just haven’t been able to keep up the same way.\n\nRelationships… I don’t know if I’d say it’s destroyed anything, but it has affected how I show up for people. Before I leave the house or before I start work, I’ll mentally replay conversations, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t say something wrong or miss some serious mistake.\n\nAnd then suddenly a lot of time is gone. It’s strange, because with computers you learn to double-check things—version control, file paths, all of that—and that’s useful, but this feels different. It’s like I can’t quite get the “all clear” in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any physical actions, like checking or arranging things, in a particular sequence or order?\n\nPatient: Not in a sequence exactly, no… I don’t have, like, a left-to-right arrangement I have to do or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that I get pulled into checking the same things over and over. Re-reading an email or a text, then reopening the folder again, then going back to the message… and before I leave the house or start work, I’ll replay a conversation in my head. Like I’m trying to make absolutely sure I didn’t make some serious mistake.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, because in my line of work checking things is normal—you know, files, versions, whether something saved correctly—but this isn’t efficient checking. It’s more like I can’t move on until I feel certain, and that certainty doesn’t really come.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts and checking behaviors are causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just annoying, it’s causing real problems.\n\nMy work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, which is… embarrassing, honestly. I used to be pretty dependable with that sort of thing. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally handled reliably. I always liked mentoring, too—there’s something satisfying about helping someone understand a tricky bit of code, like when you finally see the logic click for them—but lately I haven’t been able to keep up with it the same way.\n\nAnd outside work, that youth coding workshop still bothers me. I was late and not prepared the way I should’ve been, and it mattered to me, so I felt really embarrassed. So yes, it’s distressing, and it’s affecting my daily life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts and checking behaviors are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially at work. I mean, that’s where it shows up the most clearly.\n\nI’ve slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, which is hard to admit because I’ve always sort of prided myself on being reliable with that. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle without much trouble. I like those sessions, actually—helping someone get unstuck in code, it reminds me of when I was first learning and someone took the time with me—but lately I just haven’t been able to keep up the same way.\n\nRelationships… I don’t know if I’d say it’s destroyed anything, but it has affected how I show up for people. Like that youth coding workshop—I was late and unprepared, and that really mattered to me. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to fulfill responsibilities or meet expectations in your personal or professional life due to these thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. It’s mostly professional, but it bleeds into personal responsibilities too.\n\nAt work, I’m just slower than I used to be. I’ve missed design milestones because I get stuck checking and rechecking instead of moving forward. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally would’ve handled reliably. That bothers me, because I’ve always liked being the person people could count on for that.\n\nAnd then there was this youth coding workshop—I know that’s not exactly work, but it mattered to me. I showed up late and wasn’t prepared the way I should’ve been, and I felt really embarrassed. I like those sessions, actually—helping someone get unstuck in code, it reminds me of when I was first learning and someone took the time with me—but lately I just haven’t been able to keep up the same way.\n\nRelationships… I don’t know if I’d say it’s destroyed anything, but it has affected how I show up for people. Like that youth coding workshop—I was late and unprepared, and that really mattered to me. I felt pretty embarrassed about that. So yes, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to fulfill responsibilities or meet expectations in your personal or professional life due to these thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. I’ve missed design milestones because I get stuck checking and rechecking instead of moving forward. And I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions that I normally would’ve handled reliably. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so, though I notice it more around responsibilities than, like, going out with friends. I’ve always been a little more work-focused anyway—software can sort of eat your life if you let it, and I guess I did let it for years.

But yes, it interferes. My work has slowed down enough that I’ve missed design milestones, and I’ve had to postpone mentoring sessions I used to handle pretty reliably. That part feels social in a way, because mentoring is about being present for someone, not just finishing a task.

And outside of work, the youth coding workshop really got to me. I was late and unprepared, and I felt embarrassed because that mattered to me. I don’t know if it’s damaged relationships exactly, but it has made me feel like I’m letting people down.

I kept thinking afterward, you know, these kids showed up expecting something from me, and I couldn’t quite… deliver the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have these repetitive thoughts or checking behaviors caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with people, I guess it’s less like big arguments and more that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nWork has slowed down a lot because of all the rechecking, and I’ve missed design milestones.
